56:["$","p",null,{"children":["The Pauli Propagation Method (PPM) provides a noiseless classical simulation of the quantum circuit by back-propagating measured observables through the circuit in the Heisenberg picture. Under Clifford layers (CNOT, H, S, X gates), Pauli operators map to other Pauli operators without increasing the number of terms. 5a:["$","p",null,{"children":["The ",["$","code",null,{"children":"atol","className":"text-code-02 inline px-4 bg-background border border-transparent dark:border-border-subtle-00 [overflow-wrap:anywhere] group-[.pre-el]:text-code-02 group-[.pre-el]:inline-block group-[.pre-el]:whitespace-pre group-[.pre-el]:p-12 group-[.pre-el]:pr-[50px] group-[.pre-el]:bg-inherit dark:group-[.pre-el]:border-transparent group-[.paragraph-el]:whitespace-normal"}]," parameter in ",["$","code",null,{"children":"propagate_through_circuit","className":"text-code-02 inline px-4 bg-background border border-transparent dark:border-border-subtle-00 [overflow-wrap:anywhere] group-[.pre-el]:text-code-02 group-[.pre-el]:inline-block group-[.pre-el]:whitespace-pre group-[.pre-el]:p-12 group-[.pre-el]:pr-[50px] group-[.pre-el]:bg-inherit dark:group-[.pre-el]:border-transparent group-[.paragraph-el]:whitespace-normal"}]," controls how aggressively small Pauli branches are pruned. A very tight threshold (for example, ",["$","code",null,{"children":"1e-12","className":"text-code-02 inline px-4 bg-background border border-transparent dark:border-border-subtle-00 [overflow-wrap:anywhere] group-[.pre-el]:text-code-02 group-[.pre-el]:inline-block group-[.pre-el]:whitespace-pre group-[.pre-el]:p-12 group-[.pre-el]:pr-[50px] group-[.pre-el]:bg-inherit dark:group-[.pre-el]:border-transparent group-[.paragraph-el]:whitespace-normal"}],") retains nearly all branches and gives exact results, but simulation time grows steeply with circuit depth; the 120-qubit simulation in the ",["$","$L2",null,{"href":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18080","children":"paper"}]," took approximately 8.5 hours with the default settings. Raising the threshold (for example, to ",["$","code",null,{"children":"1e-6","className":"text-code-02 inline px-4 bg-background border border-transparent dark:border-border-subtle-00 [overflow-wrap:anywhere] group-[.pre-el]:text-code-02 group-[.pre-el]:inline-block group-[.pre-el]:whitespace-pre group-[.pre-el]:p-12 group-[.pre-el]:pr-[50px] group-[.pre-el]:bg-inherit dark:group-[.pre-el]:border-transparent group-[.paragraph-el]:whitespace-normal"}]," or ",["$","code",null,{"children":"1e-3","className":"text-code-02 inline px-4 bg-background border border-transparent dark:border-border-subtle-00 [overflow-wrap:anywhere] group-[.pre-el]:text-code-02 group-[.pre-el]:inline-block group-[.pre-el]:whitespace-pre group-[.pre-el]:p-12 group-[.pre-el]:pr-[50px] group-[.pre-el]:bg-inherit dark:group-[.pre-el]:border-transparent group-[.paragraph-el]:whitespace-normal"}],") discards terms whose coefficients fall below that value, dramatically reducing the number of tracked terms and speeding up computation. The trade-off is a small, controllable approximation error that you can validate by comparing results at different thresholds."],"className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
